Appendix B: Circular Economy
The circular economy is an alternative approach to the traditional ‘take > make > use > This Appendix outlines a circular economy approach that demonstrates opportunities to 
dispose’ model whereby raw materials are collected, transformed into products, used, and incorporate circular economy principles at each stage of a development project. 
then discarded as waste. As well as resulting in value being lost at product end-of-life, this Adopting the principles set out in this approach into projects will support performance 
approach also produces a range of negative externalities that include resource scarcity, against the DSP Themes Whole Life Carbon, and Circular Economy, Waste and Water. The 
illegal waste practices, human health concerns from hazardous waste, unsustainable levels Sustainability Coordinator should work with the design team to identify opportunities to 
of water extraction, rising carbon emissions, and widespread ecosystem pollution. adopt these principles. The figure below shows how this approach overlays with the RIBA 
In the circular economy the value of products and materials are retained, eliminating the work stages.
production of waste and reducing embodied carbon in construction projects. The circular 
economy follows the waste mitigation hierarchy which orders approaches from most to 
least impactful considering retention of resource value and waste reduction, as shown in 0. Site  1. Strategic 2. Circular 3. Circular 4. Circular 5. Circular 
the figure below. Evaluation Brief Design Procurement Construction Operation

42 Development Sustainability Principles



Circular Economy Design Approach Principles

Circular Construction Offsite construction 
Construct buildings in a way that minimises Contractor to complete elements of the building works in a factory setting. Presents the opportunity to reduce 
waste products. waste and recycle pre-consumer materials in production.

Reuse 
Contractor to use second-life materials for the temporary works or site installation.
Waste management 
Contractor to send back unused materials to suppliers and waste streams segregated and stored for reuse, 
take-back or recycling.
Disassembly sequencing 
Contractor to plan for disassembly sequence to time and cost and improve material recovery at end of life.
Industry 4.0 
Contractor to review digitalisation of construction industry and the automation of processes, includes 3D 
printing, material passports, blockchain and Artificial Intelligence.

Circular Operation  Raising awareness 
Reduce the quantity of waste generated  Operations to raise awareness with building occupants on the benefits of reducing the volume of products and 
in operations and ensuring material value materials being consumed and best practice to manage those that are.
 is retained. Product leasing 

Operations to review product leasing as a model by which products are only used when they are needed, thereby 
reducing downtime and the overall count of each.
Maintenance and repair 
Operations to maintain and repair to preserve the condition of products.
Selling and donating 
Operations to sell or donate products and materials no longer needed thereby maintaining their life in their 
existing form. This can be done through reuse brokers or directly to businesses and charities.
Upcycling 
Operations to upcycle, this is the process of taking products and adding life and value to them by replacing 
components or re-engineering their look and function.
Return to manufacturer 
Operations to return products and materials to the manufacturer to be re-engineered or recycled. Some 
materials and products can be returned to the manufacturer if the product is faulty, broken, damaged, unusable, 
does not match what was advertised or is simply at end of life.

as possible in-situ; this will also reduce the embodied carbon of the proposed building. building to be carefully stripped out so that materials can be recovered and reused

feasibility and associated carbon emission with any repair process should be considered. audit should:

Floor coverings (soft) Carpets, vinyl flooring

Architectural features Roof tiles, reclaimed bricks, fireplaces

Mixed/other Efforts should be made to categorise waste into the above categories wherever possible

45 Development Sustainability Principles



Appendix C: Material Principles
The Crown Estate is committed to the sustainable and responsible use of materials.  This policy sets out the Key Principles 
and specific commitments to be followed by our supply chain.

Key Principles:
We aim to select materials that are: 

Safe	 Avoiding worker risks such as child/bonded labour and unsafe working conditions in the supply chain  
in line with relevant legislation.

Healthy	 Supporting the health of both building occupants and the workers who install, maintain and 
decommission them.

Low impact	 Avoiding habitat destruction and damage to the natural environment as a result of material sourcing and 
production.

Non-polluting	 Avoiding release of harmful substances that damage the surrounding environment (including the air and 
water sources) as a result of material extraction, processing, or use in operation.

Low-emitting	 Minimising CO2 and other greenhouse gas emissions resulting from material sourcing, including 
emissions released from mining and processing.

Traceable	 With high visibility and/or traceability through the supply chain.
Circular	 .Minimising resource use and selecting materials that are durable, reusable, recyclable or rapidly 

renewable.

requirements. The team is expected to work together collaboratively and take shared responsibility for the successful delivery of a 
sustainable scheme. The key roles performed by the different members of the design team are summarised below.
Full duties and specific consultant responsibilities will be confirmed by The Crown Estate on a project-by-project basis.

Climate Resilience

The electrical resilience of the building is as follows:
• Utility supply at MV in standard RMU configuration
• Options still considered are connections from two separate primary substations 
and Solkor Unit protection for highest security of supply
• The LV system has been separated with two primary switchboards and two 
separate landlord transformers all in their 120min fire separated compartments
• The combined landlord and life safety generator has capacity to supply up to 
80% of the office building load whilst load shedding the Retail unts.
• The primary switchgear is interconnected for resilience.

Incorporate measures within the design • The transformers are sized at full building load individually i.e. a single 
to future proof the development to Transformer can supply the complete building.
maintain energy and water security, and • The primary switchgear is separately connected to the generator/ life safety 

Utility Resilience Yes Green Green
business continuity during potential switchboard providing high backup resilience to individual primary switch boards
periods of disruption to supplies e.g. 
droughts and power cuts

Water services are designed as follows:
• Office and retail have individual Thames Water supplies.
• We have a cold-water storage tank sized at BS total office building occupancy
• We have rainwater harvesting storage and day tank capacity for WC flushing 
and irrigation, providing approx. 7-8 days capacity without top-up, assuming full 
when failure occurs.
• Due to this setup, we have an approx. increased office building resiliency of 10-
15% in the water storage system without oversizing the storage tank.
• The pump setups are minimum run and standby, with generator backup
